## DAY 3 PM — TRANSMISSION LINES + PER UNIT SYSTEM

---

## FORMULA 1: Transmission Line Parameters

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Why do we need transmission lines?**

```
Power is generated at 11-33 kV (convenient for generators)
Power is used at 400V-11 kV (convenient for consumers)

But: P = V × I
  For same power, HIGHER voltage → LOWER current
  Lower current → thinner wires → less cost

So we TRANSMIT at high voltage (400 kV, 220 kV, 132 kV)
Then step DOWN for distribution.

India's grid:
  Generation: 11 kV (generators)
  Primary Transmission: 400 kV (state grids)
  Secondary Transmission: 220 kV, 132 kV
  Distribution: 33 kV, 11 kV
  Consumer: 415V (three-phase), 240V (single-phase)
```

**Line Parameters (per phase per km):**

```
Resistance (R):
  R = ρ / A  (ohm per km)
  ρ = resistivity of conductor material
  A = cross-sectional area

Inductance (L):
  L = 2 × 10⁻⁷ × ln(D/r')  H/km
  D = distance between conductors (geometric mean distance)
  r' = effective radius (GMR = Geometric Mean Radius)
  r' = r × e^(μ/4π) × e^(-1/4) for solid conductor

Capacitance (C):
  C = 2πε / ln(D/r)  F/km
  ε = permittivity of air
  D = distance between conductors
  r = radius of conductor
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

**Indian transmission lines:**

```
1. 400 kV LINES (major inter-state):
   → ACSR (Aluminum Conductor Steel Reinforced)
   → Bundle conductors (2 or 4 per phase)
   → Horizontal configuration
   → Typical span: 300-400 meters

2. 220 kV LINES (state transmission):
   → ACSR conductors
   → Single or bundle conductors
   → Typical span: 250-350 meters

3. 132 kV LINES (sub-transmission):
   → ACSR or AAAC
   → Single conductor
   → Typical span: 200-300 meters

4. 33 kV LINES (rural distribution):
   → Single pole structures
   → Longer spans
   → Higher losses (lower voltage)

Parameters per km for typical 400 kV line:
  R ≈ 0.03-0.05 Ω/km
  L ≈ 1.0-1.2 mH/km
  C ≈ 0.01-0.012 μF/km
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Resistance:**
$$R = \frac{\rho}{A} \text{ Ω/km}$$

**Inductance:**
$$L = 2 \times 10^{-7} \times \ln\left(\frac{D}{r'}\right) \text{ H/km}$$

**Capacitance:**
$$C = \frac{2\pi\varepsilon_0}{\ln(D/r)} \text{ F/km}$$

Where:
| Symbol | Value |
|--------|-------|
| ρ (Aluminum) | 2.82 × 10⁻⁸ Ω-m |
| ε₀ | 8.854 × 10⁻¹² F/m |
| r' (GMR) | r × e^(-μ/4π) ≈ r × 0.7788 |

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "Inductance increases with conductor radius" | Actually L ∝ ln(D/r) — larger r → smaller L |
| 2 | "Capacitance increases with spacing" | Actually C ∝ 1/ln(D/r) — larger D → smaller C |
| 3 | "GMR = physical radius" | No, GMR < physical radius |
| 4 | "D = distance between centers" | Use geometric mean distance for 3-phase |

### STEP 5 — Practice Questions:

**Q1 (APTRANSCO 2018):** Calculate the inductance of a 3-phase transmission line with conductors spaced 5m apart in equilateral formation. Conductor radius is 1.5 cm.

**Solution:**
```
Given:
  D = 5 m (equilateral spacing)
  r = 0.015 m

GMR r' = r × 0.7788 = 0.015 × 0.7788 = 0.01168 m

L = 2 × 10⁻⁷ × ln(D/r') H/m
  = 2 × 10⁻⁷ × ln(5/0.01168) × 1000 m
  = 2 × 10⁻⁴ × ln(427.4)
  = 2 × 10⁻⁴ × 6.057
  = 1.21 × 10⁻³ H/km
  = 1.21 mH/km ✓
```

---

## FORMULA 2: Per Unit System

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Why per unit?**

```
In power systems, we have:
  → Voltages: 400 kV, 220 kV, 132 kV, 33 kV, 11 kV, 415V...
  → Currents: from amps to thousands of amps
  → Impedances: from milliohms to kiloohms
  → Powers: from kW to GW

It's hard to work with all these numbers.
PER UNIT simplifies everything!

Choose BASE values:
  → Base MVA (S_base) — usually 100 MVA or 10 MVA
  → Base kV (V_base) — the voltage level you're working at

Then:
  Z_pu = Z_actual / Z_base
  I_pu = I_actual / I_base
  V_pu = V_actual / V_base

Where:
  Z_base = V_base² / S_base
  I_base = S_base / V_base
```

**Changing bases:**

```
A device has Z_pu on its OWN rating.
We need Z on SYSTEM base.

Z_new = Z_old × (MVA_new / MVA_old) × (kV_old / kV_new)²

This is crucial when combining different components.
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

**Per unit in Indian grid studies:**

```
Per unit is used in ALL power system analysis:

1. LOAD FLOW studies:
  → All bus voltages in pu
  → Line flows in pu
  → Typical base: 100 MVA or 500 MVA

2. FAULT calculations:
  → Fault currents in pu
  → Convert back to kA using I_base

3. STABILITY studies:
  → All machine parameters in pu
  → Compare different machines easily

4. TRANSFORMER studies:
  → Each winding has different voltage
  → Per unit keeps it consistent
  → Z_pu is same for all windings when referred
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Base Quantities:**
$$S_{base} \text{ (MVA)}, V_{base} \text{ (kV at that level)}$$
$$Z_{base} = \frac{V_{base}^2}{S_{base}} \text{ Ω}$$
$$I_{base} = \frac{S_{base}}{\sqrt{3} \times V_{base}} \text{ kA}$$

**Per Unit Conversion:**
$$Z_{pu} = \frac{Z_{actual}}{Z_{base}} = Z_{actual} \times \frac{S_{base}}{V_{base}^2}$$

**Changing Base:**
$$Z_{new} = Z_{old} \times \left(\frac{MVA_{new}}{MVA_{old}}\right) \times \left(\frac{kV_{old}}{kV_{new}}\right)^2$$

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "Use wrong voltage for base" | Must use voltage AT THAT level (line-to-line) |
| 2 | "Forget to convert kV to V" | Keep units consistent! |
| 3 | "Different base MVA for different components" | Convert ALL to same base |
| 4 | "Forgetting the squared term" | kV² appears in Z_base |

### STEP 5 — Practice Questions:

**Q1 (APPSC 2018):** A transformer has 10% impedance on its own rating of 20 MVA, 11 kV/132 kV. Find Z in ohms on a 100 MVA base.

**Solution:**
```
Given:
  Z_pu_own = 0.10 pu on 20 MVA, 11 kV base (LV side)
  We need to find Z on 100 MVA base at same 11 kV

Z_base_old = V²/S = 11²/20 = 121/20 = 6.05 Ω (LV side)

Z_actual = Z_pu × Z_base = 0.10 × 6.05 = 0.605 Ω

Z_base_new = 11²/100 = 121/100 = 1.21 Ω

Z_pu_new = Z_actual / Z_base_new = 0.605 / 1.21 = 0.50 pu

Or using formula:
  Z_new = Z_old × (MVA_new/MVA_old) × (kV_old/kV_new)²
  Z_new = 0.10 × (100/20) × (11/11)²
  Z_new = 0.10 × 5 × 1 = 0.50 pu ✓
```


## FORMULA 3: Bus Impedance & Admittance Matrices

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Why bus matrices?**
```
In a power system with N buses, the relationship between bus voltages and injected currents:
  I_bus = Y_bus × V_bus   (admittance form)
  V_bus = Z_bus × I_bus   (impedance form)

Y_bus = [Y_ij] where:
  Y_ii = sum of admittances connected to bus i (self-admittance)
  Y_ij = -y_ij (mutual admittance between bus i and j)
  y_ij = 1/Z_ij = admittance of line between bus i and j

Z_bus = Y_bus⁻¹  (inverse of Y_bus)
```

**Building Y_bus (by inspection):**
```
For a 3-bus system with lines Z_12, Z_13, Z_23:
  Y_11 = 1/Z_12 + 1/Z_13 + (shunt at bus 1)
  Y_22 = 1/Z_12 + 1/Z_23 + (shunt at bus 2)
  Y_33 = 1/Z_13 + 1/Z_23 + (shunt at bus 3)
  Y_12 = Y_21 = -1/Z_12
  Y_13 = Y_31 = -1/Z_13
  Y_23 = Y_32 = -1/Z_23
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:
```
Every load flow study (Gauss-Seidel, Newton-Raphson) starts with building Y_bus.
In APTransco's control center, Y_bus is updated every time a line switches.
Z_bus is used for fault analysis: fault current at bus k = 1/Z_kk(pu).
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:
```
Y_ii = sum of all admittances incident at bus i (including shunt)
Y_ij = -y_ij (for i ≠ j, where line exists)
Y_bus is symmetric (Y_ij = Y_ji) for passive networks
Z_bus = Y_bus⁻¹ (can be built directly by adding elements one at a time)
```

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:
```
| Tweak | Trap | Counter |
|-------|------|---------|
| Mutual coupling between lines | Y_bus modification needed | Coupling adds off-diagonal terms |
| Phase-shifting transformer | Y_bus not symmetric | Non-reciprocal phase shift breaks symmetry |
| Y_bus singularity | Isolated bus or no reference | Connect at least one line to swing bus |
```

---

## DAY 3 PM — REVISION CARD

```
### 📝 PYQs — Transmission Lines & Per Unit System

**Q1 (APGENCO 2012):** The surge impedance of a transmission line is given by:
(a) √(L/C)  (b) √(LC)  (c) 1/√(LC)  (d) √(C/L)

**Solution:** (a) √(L/C). Surge impedance Z₀ = √(z/y) = √(jwL/jwC) = √(L/C) for lossless line.

**Q2 (Transco 2012):** Ferranti effect occurs in:
(a) Short transmission lines  (b) Medium transmission lines
(c) Long transmission lines at no-load  (d) Under-ground cables only

**Solution:** (c) Long transmission lines at no-load — receiving end voltage > sending end voltage due to charging current.

**Q3 (EPDCL 2010):** The undesirable effects of corona are:
(a) Power loss  (b) Radio interference  (c) Harmonics  (d) All of these

**Solution:** (d) All of these — Corona causes power loss, radio interference, and harmonic generation.

**Q4 (APGENCO 2017):** In a per-unit system, the base impedance for a 100 MVA, 220 kV system is:
(a) 220 Ω  (b) 484 Ω  (c) 100 Ω  (d) 4840 Ω

**Solution:** (b) 484 Ω — Z_base = V²/S = 220²/100 = 48400/100 = 484 Ω.

**Q5 (TSGenco 2015):** Skin effect in transmission lines causes:
(a) Increase in effective resistance  (b) Decrease in inductance
(c) Increase in capacitance  (d) No change

**Solution:** (a) Increase in effective resistance — current concentrates at conductor surface → less cross-section → higher R_ac.

**Q6 (APGENCO 2020):** In a suspension insulator, the maximum voltage appears across the disc:
(a) Nearest to the conductor  (b) Nearest to the cross-arm
(c) At the middle  (d) Uniformly distributed

**Solution:** (a) Nearest to the conductor — due to capacitive voltage distribution, the disc nearest the conductor has highest voltage drop.


==============================================================================
                       TRANSMISSION + PER UNIT -- DAY 3 PM
==============================================================================

1. LINE PARAMETERS:
   R = rho / A  (ohm/km)
   L = 2x10^-7 x ln(D/r') H/km
   C = 2 x pi x epsilon / ln(D/r) F/km

2. PER UNIT SYSTEM:
   Z_base = V^2 / S (ohm)
   Z_pu = Z_actual / Z_base
   Z_new = Z_old x (MVA_new/MVA_old) x (kV_old/kV_new)^2

3. LINE MODELS:
   Short (<80 km): R + jX only
   Medium: R + jX, with shunt admittance
   Long: Distributed parameters

4. VOLTAGE CONTROL:
   VS = VR + IZ (series drop)
   Shunt capacitor: reduces voltage drop
==============================================================================
```

<a id="subject-7-fault-analysis-symmetrical-components"></a>
## DAY 4 AM — FAULT ANALYSIS + SYMMETRICAL COMPONENTS

---

## FORMULA 1: Symmetrical Components

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Why do we need symmetrical components?**

```
When a fault occurs, currents become ASYMMETRICAL (unbalanced).
Analyzing unbalanced 3-phase systems is very difficult.

SYMMETRICAL COMPONENTS breaks one unbalanced system into
THREE balanced systems:

  1. POSITIVE SEQUENCE:      3 phasors, 120° apart, same magnitude
                              Rotates in normal direction (R-Y-B)

  2. NEGATIVE SEQUENCE:      3 phasors, 120° apart, same magnitude
                              Rotates in opposite direction (R-B-Y)

  3. ZERO SEQUENCE:          3 phasors, all in PHASE (0° apart)
                              No rotation
```

**The 'a' operator:**

```
'a' = 1 ∠ 120° = -0.5 + j0.866

Properties:
  a² = 1 ∠ 240° = -0.5 - j0.866
  1 + a + a² = 0
  a³ = 1

Useful relations:
  a² = a' (complex conjugate of a)
  a - a² = j√3
  1 - a = (3/2) - j(√3/2)
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

**Faults in Indian power system:**

```
Common faults and their sequence components:

1. THREE-PHASE FAULT (3-φ to ground):
   → Symmetrical fault
   → Only POSITIVE sequence
   → Most severe but rarest (5%)

2. LINE-TO-GROUND FAULT (L-G):
   → Most common (70-80% of faults)
   → All three sequences present
   → Usually caused by lightning or conductor break

3. LINE-TO-LINE FAULT (L-L):
   → Second most common (15-20%)
   → Positive and negative sequences only
   → Usually caused by conductor clash

4. DOUBLE LINE-TO-GROUND (L-L-G):
   → All three sequences
   → Most complex

Indian Grid:
  → 400 kV lines prone to lightning
  → L-G faults most common
  → Protection: distance relays, differential relays
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Transformation (using matrix):**

$$[\begin{pmatrix}V_0\\V_1\\V_2\end{pmatrix}] = \frac{1}{3}[\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\1&a&a^2\\1&a^2&a\end{pmatrix}][\begin{pmatrix}V_R\\V_Y\\V_B\end{pmatrix}]$$

**Inverse:**
$$[\begin{pmatrix}V_R\\V_Y\\V_B\end{pmatrix}] = [\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\1&a^2&a\\1&a&a^2\end{pmatrix}][\begin{pmatrix}V_0\\V_1\\V_2\end{pmatrix}]$$

**Sequence Currents:**
$$I_0 = (I_R + I_Y + I_B)/3$$
$$I_1 = (I_R + aI_Y + a^2I_B)/3$$
$$I_2 = (I_R + a^2I_Y + aI_B)/3$$

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "Zero sequence current in balanced load" | Always zero for balanced conditions |
| 2 | "Zero sequence in delta connection" | Delta doesn't draw zero sequence current |
| 3 | "a² vs a" in formula | Be careful: V₁ uses a, V₂ uses a² |
| 4 | "L-L-G vs L-G" | L-L-G has two phases to ground, more complex |

### STEP 5 — Practice Questions:

**Q1 (APPSC 2017):** Calculate the symmetrical components of I_R = 10∠0°, I_Y = 10∠-120°, I_B = 10∠120°.

**Solution:**
```
These are BALANCED phasors (R-Y-B sequence, equal magnitude).

I₁ = (I_R + aI_Y + a²I_B)/3
  a = 1∠120°, a² = 1∠240°
  
  I₁ = (10∠0 + a×10∠-120 + a²×10∠120) / 3
  = (10 + 10∠0 + 10∠0) / 3   ← since a×1∠-120 = 1∠0, etc.
  = (10 + 10 + 10) / 3
  = 30/3 = 10∠0° pu

I₂ = (I_R + a²I_Y + aI_B)/3
  = (10∠0 + a²×10∠-120 + a×10∠120) / 3
  = (10 + 10∠240 + 10∠240) / 3 ← wait

Actually: a²×1∠-120 = 1∠240 × 1∠-120 = 1∠120
         a×1∠120 = 1∠120 × 1∠120 = 1∠240
  
  I₂ = (10 + 10∠120 + 10∠240) / 3 = 0

I₀ = (I_R + I_Y + I_B) / 3 = 0

So this balanced set has only positive sequence ✓
```

---

## FORMULA 2: Fault Current Calculations

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Sequence Networks:**

```
Each sequence component has its own network:

POSITIVE SEQUENCE:
  → Same as Thevenin impedance of the system
  → Z₁ is what we use for 3-φ faults
  → Contains generators, transformers, lines

NEGATIVE SEQUENCE:
  → Same as positive sequence for most equipment
  → Z₂ ≈ Z₁ (generators are exception)
  → Only present during UNBALANCED faults

ZERO SEQUENCE:
  → Depends on grounding
  → In transformer: path depends on winding connection
  → Y grounded: zero sequence current can flow
  → Δ: zero sequence blocked
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

**Indian protection systems:**

```
In a 400 kV substation:
  → Circuit breakers rated for 40 kA fault current
  → Protection clears fault in 80-100 ms (cycles)

Fault current levels:
  → 400 kV: up to 40 kA
  → 220 kV: up to 40 kA  
  → 132 kV: up to 31 kA
  → 33 kV: up to 25 kA

When fault occurs:
  → Currents can be 10-20× normal
  → Protection must detect and isolate quickly
  → Breaker clears fault, rest of grid continues
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Fault Currents by Type:**

| Fault Type | Formula | Sequences |
|------------|---------|-----------|
| 3-φ | $I_f = V/Z_1$ | Z₁ only |
| L-L | $I_f = V\sqrt{3}/(Z_1+Z_2)$ | Z₁ and Z₂ |
| L-G | $I_f = 3V/(Z_0+Z_1+Z_2)$ | Z₀, Z₁, Z₂ |
| L-L-G | Complex | All three |

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "L-L-G is same as L-G" | NO — L-L-G involves TWO phases to ground |
| 2 | "3-φ fault is most severe" | Actually L-G can be similar, but 3-φ is symmetrical |
| 3 | "Using wrong sequence network" | Match fault type to correct sequence combination |

### STEP 5 — Practice Questions:

**Q1 (APGENCO 2019):** A 3-φ bus has fault level of 1000 MVA at 11 kV. Find the fault current.

**Solution:**
```
Given: S_fault = 1000 MVA, V = 11 kV

S_fault = √3 × V × I_f
I_f = S_fault / (√3 × V)
    = 1000 × 10⁶ / (1.732 × 11 × 10³)
    = 1000000 / 19052
    = 52.5 kA

Alternatively, per unit:
  I_base = S_base / (√3 × V)
  I_f_pu = S_fault/S_base
  I_f = I_f_pu × I_base
```

---

## DAY 4 AM — REVISION CARD

```
### 📝 PYQs — Fault Analysis

**Q1 (APGENCO 2012):** A 3-phase fault current in a power system depends on:
(a) Positive sequence impedance only  (b) Positive + negative sequence
(c) All three sequences  (d) Zero sequence only

**Solution:** (a) Positive sequence impedance only — 3-φ fault is balanced → only Z₁ carries current.

**Q2 (Transco 2012):** For a line-to-line fault in a power system, the fault current involves:
(a) Z₁ only  (b) Z₁ + Z₂  (c) Z₁ + Z₂ + Z₀  (d) Z₀ only

**Solution:** (b) Z₁ + Z₂ — L-L fault has no zero-sequence path (no ground involved). I_f = √3·V/(Z₁+Z₂).

**Q3 (HMWS 2015):** A 100 MVA, 11kV generator has X₁ = 0.2 pu. The 3-phase fault current at the generator terminals is:
(a) 26.2 kA  (b) 52.5 kA  (c) 13.1 kA  (d) 5.25 kA

**Solution:** (a) 26.2 kA — I_base = 100×10⁶/(√3×11×10³) = 5249 A. I_f(pu) = 1/Z₁ = 1/0.2 = 5 pu. I_f = 5×5249 = 26,245 A ≈ 26.2 kA.

**Q4 (APGENCO 2020):** For a single line-to-ground fault in a solidly grounded system:
(a) All three sequence networks are in series  (b) All three are in parallel
(c) Only positive is active  (d) Positive and negative are in parallel

**Solution:** (a) All three sequence networks are in series — SLG fault: Z₁+Z₂+Z₀ in series. I_f = 3V/(Z₁+Z₂+Z₀).

**Q5 (TSGenco 2015):** The zero-sequence current flows only when:
(a) The neutral is grounded  (b) The fault involves ground
(c) Both (a) and (b)  (d) The system is ungrounded

**Solution:** (c) Both (a) and (b) — zero-sequence current requires a ground path and a ground fault.


==============================================================================
                            FAULT ANALYSIS -- DAY 4 AM
==============================================================================

1. SYMMETRICAL COMPONENTS:
   a = 1 at 120 deg = -0.5 + j0.866
   a^2 = 1 at 240 deg,  1 + a + a^2 = 0
   V0 = (VR + VY + VB) / 3
   V1 = (VR + a x VY + a^2 x VB) / 3
   V2 = (VR + a^2 x VY + a x VB) / 3

2. FAULT FORMULAS:
   3-phi: If = V / Z1
   L-L: If = 1.732 x V / (Z1 + Z2)
   L-G: If = 3 x V / (Z0 + Z1 + Z2)

3. SEQUENCE NETWORKS:
   Positive: Same as Thevenin
   Negative: approx Z1 for most equipment
   Zero: Depends on grounding (Y allows, Delta blocks)
==============================================================================
```

<a id="subject-8-protection-stability-economic-dispatch"></a>
## DAY 4 PM — PROTECTION + STABILITY + ECONOMIC DISPATCH

---

## FORMULA 1: Circuit Breakers + Protection

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Why do we need protection?**

```
When fault occurs:
  → Current becomes HUGE (10-50× normal)
  → Equipment heats up quickly
  → Mechanical forces damage conductors
  → Voltage dips affect entire system

Protection = detect fault + isolate quickly + minimize damage

The PROTECTION ZONE concept:
  Each equipment (generator, transformer, line) has its own protection
  Zones overlap slightly to ensure no blind spots

Protection must be:
  1. FAST — clear fault before damage
  2. SELECTIVE — isolate only the faulty section
  3. SENSITIVE — detect even small faults
  4. RELIABLE — works when needed
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

**Protection in Indian grid:**

```
400 kV Substation Protection:
  → Each line protected by TWO independent relays
  → Primary: Distance relay (zone 1, 2, 3)
  → Backup: Overcurrent relay
  → Breaker: SF6 gas, 4000 A, 40 kA

Protection philosophy:
  → If line fault: trip both ends quickly (80 ms)
  → If bus fault: trip all feeders (100 ms)
  → If transformer fault: trip transformer + bus (200 ms)

Types of relays:
  → Electromechanical (old, but still used)
  → Static (transistor-based)
  → Numerical (microprocessor-based) — modern
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Circuit Breaker Rating:**
$$I_{break} = \frac{S_{break}}{\sqrt{3} \times V}$$

**Making Current:**
$$I_{making} = 2.55 \times I_{break}$$

**Protection Zones:**
| Zone | Reach | Time |
|------|-------|------|
| Zone 1 | 80% of line | Instantaneous |
| Zone 2 | 120% of line + 50% of next | 0.3-0.5s |
| Zone 3 | 100% of next + 50% of next-next | Backup |

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "Zone 1 is 100% of line" | NO — 80%, to avoid hitting end through overlap |
| 2 | "SF6 is toxic" | Inert, good dielectric, but PD protocols needed |
| 3 | "Breaker rated current vs breaking current" | Current: continuous rating; kA: fault level |

---

## FORMULA 2: Power System Stability

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**What is stability?**

```
A power system is STABLE if, after a disturbance:
  → All generators return to SYNCHRONISM
  → Voltages and frequencies return to normal

Types of stability:

1. STEADY-STATE STABILITY:
   → Small, slow disturbances
   → System adjusts gradually
   → Usually not a problem

2. TRANSIENT STABILITY:
   → LARGE disturbance (fault, sudden load change)
   → Machines swing against each other
   → Must stay in synchronism for 1-5 seconds

3. VOLTAGE STABILITY:
   → Load increases beyond transfer capability
   → Voltage collapses
   → Not about frequency/synchronism
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

**India's grid stability challenges:**

```
India's grid (POSOCO):
  → 400 kV links between states
  → Large power transfers North→South, East→West
  → Maintaining stability is critical

August 2012 blackout (north India):
  → 400 kV line tripped
  → Other lines overloaded
  → Cascading failure
  → 600 million without power

How India prevents blackouts:
  → AFC (Automatic Frequency Control)
  → Grid containment reserves
  → Under-frequency load shedding
  → Rate of change of frequency protection (RoCoF)
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Swing Equation:**
$$\frac{2H}{\omega_s} \cdot \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e$$

Where:
| Symbol | Name |
|--------|------|
| H | Inertia constant (MW-s/MVA) |
| δ | Power angle (rad) |
| Pm | Mechanical input |
| Pe | Electrical output = (EV/X)sinδ |

**Equal Area Criterion:**
```
If Area A1 (accelerating) = Area A2 (decelerating):
  → System is STABLE

If A1 > A2:
  → System is UNSTABLE
  → δ keeps increasing → loss of synchronism
```

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "Stable = no oscillation" | Transient: some oscillation OK, must damp out |
| 2 | "Equal area means equal" | Means A1 = A2 for STABLE |
| 3 | "Fault clearing time" | Faster clearing → more stable |

### STEP 5 — Practice Questions:

**Q1 (APGENCO 2018):** Explain equal area criterion for transient stability.

**Solution:**
```
EQUAL AREA CRITERION:

After a fault:
  1. Mechanical power Pm is suddenly reduced (if fault reduces electrical output)
  2. Mechanical power Pm > Electrical power Pe
  3. Rotor ACCELERATES (gains kinetic energy)

When fault clears:
  1. Electrical power suddenly increases
  2. Now Pe > Pm
  3. Rotor DECELERATES (loses kinetic energy)

The area between Pm and Pe curve on δ-ω diagram:
  A1 = acceleration area (during fault)
  A2 = deceleration area (after fault)

STABLE: A1 = A2 (energy absorbed = energy released)
UNSTABLE: A1 > A2 (system has excess kinetic energy, δ keeps increasing)
```

---

## FORMULA 3: Neutral Earthing & Insulators

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Why do we ground the neutral?**
```
Neutral grounding provides:
  1. Reference point for phase voltages
  2. Path for ground fault current to flow
  3. Protection against overvoltages
  4. Safety for personnel and equipment
```

**Methods of neutral earthing:**
```
1. SOLID EARTHING (Z0 ≈ 0):
   → Most common for HV transmission (>33 kV)
   → High SLG fault current → fast protection
   → Requires high-rated breakers

2. RESISTANCE EARTHING (Z0 = 3Rn):
   → Limits SLG fault current to safe level
   → Used in generators (limits damage to stator core)
   → Low, medium, or high resistance types

3. REACTANCE EARTHING (Z0 = j3Xn):
   → Limited use; limits fault current reactively
   
4. UNGROUNDED (Z0 = ∞):
   → No path for SLG current
   → System capacitance provides small capacitive current
   → Difficult to detect first ground fault
   
5. PETERSON COIL (ARC SUPPRESSION COIL):
   → Tuned inductor between neutral and ground
   → Cancels line-to-ground capacitance at fault point
   → Extinguishes arcing ground faults automatically
   → Used in EHV systems (220 kV, 400 kV)
```

**Effect on zero-sequence impedance:**
| Earthing | Z0 in sequence network | SLG fault current |
|---|---|---|
| Solid | Small (≈ Z1) | HIGH |
| Resistance | Zgen,0 + 3Rn | Intermediate |
| Ungrounded | ∞ | Zero (capacitive only) |
| Peterson coil | Zgen,0 + j(3XL - XC) | Near zero (tuned) |

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

```
In APTransco's 220 kV substations:
  → Transformer neutrals are solidly earthed
  → Generator neutrals are resistance-earthed (limits stator damage)
  → 400 kV lines sometimes use Peterson coils
  
Every distribution transformer (11kV/415V) in India:
  → Star side neutral is solidly earthed (provides safe 240V)
  → Earth resistance < 5Ω per IE rules
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Effect of earthing on Z0 (in sequence network):**
- Solid: Z0 = Zgen,0 (small)
- Resistance: Z0 = Zgen,0 + 3Rn
- Ungrounded: Z0 = ∞ → SLG fault current = 0

**Insulators (String Efficiency):**
- eta = V / (n × Vn) × 100%
- V = total voltage across string
- Vn = voltage across disc nearest conductor
- n = number of discs
- Efficiency IMPROVES by: longer cross-arms, grading rings, static shielding

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | "Solidly earthed → high SLG fault current" | YES — this is correct, not a problem |
| 2 | "Ungrounded — find SLG fault current" | It's capacitive: small, but can sustain |
| 3 | "Generator earthing method?" | Resistance earthing (to limit fault damage) |
| 4 | "Which disc has highest voltage drop?" | Disc nearest to conductor (bottom in suspension) |

### STEP 5 — Practice:

**Q1 (TRANSCO 2018):** A 220 kV system has transformer neutrals solidly earthed. The zero-sequence impedance per phase is 0.1 pu. Find the SLG fault current in pu and kA at a substation bus if positive and negative impedances are also 0.1 pu. Base MVA = 100.
```
For SLG fault: If = 3V / (Z0 + Z1 + Z2)
  = 3 × 1.0 / (0.1 + 0.1 + 0.1) = 3/0.3 = 10 pu
  
I_base = 100 / (√3 × 220) = 0.262 kA
If = 10 × 0.262 = 2.62 kA

If this were ungrounded: Z0 = ∞ → If ≈ 0 (just capacitive)
→ No ground path means an SLG fault is hard to detect!
```

---


## FORMULA 4: Protection of Specific Equipment

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Transformer Protection:**
```
1. BUCHHOLZ RELAY (gas-actuated):
   → Detects incipient faults (arcing produces gas)
   → Two stages: alarm (slow gas) + trip (surge)
   → Oil level also monitored
   
2. DIFFERENTIAL PROTECTION:
   → Compare currents on HV and LV sides
   → Must compensate for vector group phase shift
   → % bias characteristic: I_op > k·I_bias
   → Restraint for through-faults, operate for internal faults

3. OVERCURRENT BACKUP:
   → IDMT relay on HV side
   → Time graded with downstream protection

4. RESTRICTED EARTH FAULT (REF):
   → Sensitive ground fault detection
   → Compares neutral current with phase sum
```

**Generator Protection:**
```
1. DIFFERENTIAL PROTECTION:
   → Zone: stator windings (between neutral CTs and line CTs)
   → High impedance type for sensitive detection
   
2. LOSS OF EXCITATION:
   → Detected by distance relay (impedance enters circle on R-X plane)
   → Generator goes into induction motor mode (absorbs reactive)

3. OVERSPEED PROTECTION:
   → Mechanical governor + electronic
   → Prevents mechanical damage

4. REVERSE POWER:
   → Detects motoring (turbine loses steam)
   → Generator draws power from grid
   
5. STATOR EARTH FAULT:
   → 100% stator protection using 3rd harmonic voltage
   ```

**Transmission Line Protection:**
```
1. DISTANCE PROTECTION:
   → Mho relay (most common for long lines)
   → Zone 1: 80% (instantaneous)
   → Zone 2: 120% (0.3-0.5s delay)
   → Zone 3: 150-200% (backup, 0.8-1.0s)
   
2. PILOT RELAYING:
   → Power Line Carrier Communication (PLCC)
   → Directional comparison: both ends compare fault direction
   → Permissive overreach transfer trip (POTT)

3. AUTO-RECLOSING:
   → Single shot: close breaker once after fault clears
   → 80% of faults are transient (arc extinguishes)
   → Dead time: 0.3-1.0s for HV lines
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:
```
In APTransco's 400kV substations:
  → Each transformer has: Buchholz + differential + REF + overcurrent backup
  → Each generator unit has: stator diff + rotor earth fault + loss of excitation
  → Each line has: distance (Z1, Z2, Z3) + auto-reclose + PLCC pilot
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:
```
Bias differential: I_op = |I₁ − I₂|, I_bias = |I₁ + I₂|/2
Trip if: I_op > k·I_bias  (k = 20-80% slope)
Distance: Z_relay = V/I, plot on R-X diagram
```

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:
```
| Tweak | Trap | Counter |
|-------|------|---------|
| Transformer inrush current | Differential relay sees it as fault | 2nd harmonic restraint (inrush has high 2nd harmonic) |
| CT saturation during through-fault | False differential trip | Use high-stability CTs, bias characteristic |
| CVT transient on fault inception | Distance relay sees wrong impedance | Memory polarization or blocking |
```

## DAY 4 PM — REVISION CARD

```
### 📝 PYQs — Protection & Stability

**Q1 (APGENCO 2012):** Buchholz relay is a:
(a) Voltage sensitive device  (b) Current sensitive device
(c) Frequency sensitive device  (d) Gas actuated device

**Solution:** (d) Gas actuated device — detects transformer internal faults (arcing produces gas).

**Q2 (Transco 2012):** The making capacity of a circuit breaker is related to the breaking capacity by a factor of:
(a) 1.5  (b) 2.0  (c) 2.55  (d) 3.0

**Solution:** (c) 2.55 — I_making = 2.55 × I_breaking (accounts for peak of fully offset asymmetrical waveform).

**Q3 (HMWS 2012):** In a distance relay, Zone 1 protects:
(a) 80% of the line instantaneously  (b) 100% of the line
(c) 120% of the line with time delay  (d) The next line section

**Solution:** (a) 80% of the line instantaneously — Zone 1 reaches 80-90% with no intentional time delay.

**Q4 (APGENCO 2017):** The swing equation describes:
(a) Rotor dynamics of synchronous machines  (b) Voltage variation
(c) Current variation  (d) Power factor variation

**Solution:** (a) Rotor dynamics — (2H/ω_s)·d²δ/dt² = P_m - P_e describes the rotor angle swing.

**Q5 (TSGenco 2015):** Steady-state stability limit of a transmission line is reached when δ =:
(a) 30°  (b) 45°  (c) 90°  (d) 180°

**Solution:** (c) 90° — P = (EV/X)sinδ. dP/dδ = 0 at δ = 90°. Beyond this → synchronizing torque negative → unstable.


==============================================================================
                       PROTECTION + STABILITY -- DAY 4 PM
==============================================================================

1. PROTECTION:
   Circuit breaker: breaks fault current
   Relay: detects fault, sends trip command
   Zones: 1 (80%), 2 (120%+), 3 (backup)
   Types: overcurrent, distance, differential

2. STABILITY:
   Swing: (2H/omega_s) x d^2(delta)/dt^2 = Pm - Pe
   Equal area: A1 = A2 -> stable, A1 > A2 -> unstable
   Critical clearing angle: maximum delta before instability

3. ECONOMIC DISPATCH:
   lambda = dF/dPG (incremental fuel cost)
   At optimum: dF1/dPG1 = dF2/dPG2 = lambda
   With losses: add dP_L/dPG term
==============================================================================
```

**Solution:** (b) Fault analysis — Z_kk element gives Thevenin impedance at bus k for fault current.

**Q2 (Transco 2012):** The Buchholz relay is placed in the pipe connecting:
(a) Main tank to conservator  (b) HV bushing to tank
(c) LV winding to tap changer  (d) Radiator to tank

**Solution:** (a) Main tank to conservator — oil flows through this pipe; gas from faults collects here.

(a) 1.5  (b) 2.0  (c) 2.55  (d) 3.0

**Solution:** (c) 2.55 — I_making = 2.55 × I_breaking (peak of asymmetrical waveform).

## DAY 5 AM — POWER ELECTRONICS

---

## FORMULA 1: SCR Characteristics and Triggering

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**What is an SCR?**

```
SCR = Silicon Controlled Rectifier
     = Power switch that can be turned ON by a gate pulse
     = Cannot be turned OFF by gate (unlike MOSFET, IGBT)

Structure: 4-layer device (P-N-P-N)
  → ANODE (positive)
  → GATE (control)
  → CATHODE (negative)

How it works:
  1. Without gate pulse: blocks forward voltage
  2. With gate pulse: turns ON when forward voltage exceeds V_BO
  3. Once ON: stays ON until current drops below I_H (holding current)
  4. Gate cannot turn it OFF
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

**SCR in Indian power systems:**

```
1. HT POWER CONTROL (electric furnaces):
   → 440V, hundreds of amps
   → Phase angle control of voltage
   → Temperature control

2. DC MOTOR DRIVES:
   → Chopper circuits
   → Control DC motor speed
   → Steel rolling mills

3. BATTERY CHARGING:
   → Controlled rectifiers
   → Taper charging for lead-acid batteries
   → 12V-48V batteries

4. STATIC VAR COMPENSATORS:
   → TCR (Thyristor Controlled Reactor)
   → For reactive power control
   → Used in 400 kV substations
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**SCR Characteristics:**
| Parameter | Symbol | Typical Value |
|-----------|--------|---------------|
| Forward blocking voltage | V_DRMc | 400-2400V |
| On-state current | I_T | 100-3000A |
| Gate trigger voltage | V_GT | 2-4V |
| Gate trigger current | I_GT | 10-200 mA |
| Holding current | I_H | 5-50 mA |
| Latching current | I_L | 2-3 × I_H |

**Turn ON:**
- Apply gate pulse when anode is positive
- V_F > V_BO (forward breakover)

**Turn OFF:**
- Reduce current below I_H
- Or force current to zero (commutation)

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "SCR can be turned off by gate" | NO — needs external circuit |
| 2 | "Holding vs latching current" | I_L > I_H; I_H needed to stay ON |
| 3 | "I_GT same for all SCRs" | No — check datasheet |

### STEP 5 — Practice Questions:

**Q1 (APTRANSCO 2019):** Explain why SCR is called a "controlled" rectifier.

**Solution:**
```
SCR = Silicon Controlled Rectifier

"Rectifier" because:
  → Allows current flow in ONE direction only
  → Converts AC to DC

"Controlled" because:
  → The point at which it starts conducting can be CONTROLLED
  → Using gate pulse, you can delay when it turns ON
  → This controls the average DC voltage

Without control: Diode conducts from 0° onward ( uncontrolled)
With SCR: Conducts from α° onward ( controlled by gate)

α = firing angle = angle at which gate pulse is applied
V_dc = (V_m/π)(1 + cosα) for single-phase half-controlled

This is used to:
  → Control DC motor speed
  → Control power to heaters
  → Control battery charging
```

---

## FORMULA 2: Controlled Rectifiers

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Types of Rectifiers:**

```
1. UNCONTROLLED (diodes):
   → Fixed DC output
   → V_dc = 2V_m/π (single-phase full wave)

2. CONTROLLED (SCRs):
   → Variable DC output
   → Controlled by firing angle α
   → V_dc varies from maximum to zero

3. HALF-CONTROLLED (SCR + diode):
   → Cheaper
   → Can't achieve true DC reversal
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

**Rectifiers in Indian industry:**

```
DC motor drives (最重要的):
  → Steel rolling mills: 6-pulse rectifier, 1000V, 5000A
  → Cement mills: 6-pulse, 440V, 1000A
  → Paper mills: 12-pulse for smoother DC

Battery charging:
  → Single-phase controlled rectifier
  → Taper charging for 12V car batteries
  → Constant voltage then constant current

Electrolysis (aluminum):
  → Very large rectifiers
  → 100 kA DC at 500V
  → 12 or 24-pulse for ripple reduction
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Single-phase half-controlled:**
$$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{2\pi}(1 + \cos\alpha)$$

**Single-phase fully controlled:**
$$V_{dc} = \frac{2V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha$$

**Three-phase fully controlled:**
$$V_{dc} = \frac{3\sqrt{2}V_{LL}}{\pi}\cos\alpha$$

Where α = firing angle (0° to 90° for rectification)

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "α = 90° means full voltage" | NO — α=0° is full voltage |
| 2 | "For inversion, α > 90°" | Yes, 90° to 180° |
| 3 | "6-pulse vs 12-pulse" | 12-pulse = less ripple, needs phase-shifting transformer |

### STEP 5 — Practice Questions:

**Q1 (APPSC 2018):** A single-phase fully controlled rectifier supplies 100V DC to a load. Find the firing angle if input is 230V AC.

**Solution:**
```
Given: V_dc = 100V, V_s = 230V RMS

For single-phase fully controlled:
  V_dc = (2V_m/π) × cosα
  V_m = V_s × √2 = 230 × 1.414 = 325.3V
  
  100 = (2 × 325.3 / π) × cosα
  100 = 207.1 × cosα
  cosα = 100/207.1 = 0.483
  α = cos⁻¹(0.483) = 61.1°
```

---

## FORMULA 3: Choppers and Inverters

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Chopper = DC-DC Converter:**
```
Converts fixed DC to variable DC.

Buck (step down): V₀ = D × V_s
Boost (step up): V₀ = V_s/(1-D)

Where D = duty cycle = t_on/T
```

**Inverter = DC-AC Converter:**
```
Converts DC to AC at desired frequency.

Square wave: V_out = (4V_s/nπ) × sin(nωt)
SPWM: Pulse width modulation for cleaner sine wave
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

```
Choppers:
  → DC motor speed control (EVs, cranes)
  → SMPS (switched mode power supply)
  → Battery chargers

Inverters:
  → UPS (home, office)
  → Solar PV systems
  → Grid-tied inverters (renewable)
  → Variable frequency drives (VFD)
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Chopper:**
$$V_0 = D \times V_s \text{ (Buck)}$$
$$V_0 = \frac{V_s}{1-D} \text{ (Boost)}$$

**Inverter output voltage:**
$$V_{1(fundamental)} = \frac{4V_s}{\pi} \times \sin(\omega t) \text{ (square wave)}$$

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "D = 1 gives zero output" | NO — D=1 gives full output for buck |
| 2 | "Inverter converts AC to DC" | NO — inverter is DC to AC |

---

## FORMULA 5: SCR Turn-On & Commutation Methods

### SCR Turn-On Methods:
```
1. GATE PULSE (normal method):
   → Apply IG > IGT between gate and cathode
   → SCR latches ON (gate loses control after)
   
2. FORWARD VOLTAGE > VBO (undesired):
   → Breaks blocking region → destroys SCR
   → Prevented by proper voltage derating

3. dv/dt TRIGGERING (undesired):
   → Rapid rise in voltage causes capacitive charging of junction
   → Can trigger SCR even without gate pulse
   → Prevented by RC SNUBBER across the SCR

4. LIGHT TRIGGERING (LASCR):
   → Light activates thyristor
   → Used in HVDC (optical isolation)

5. TEMPERATURE:
   → Leakage current increases with temp
   → Can trigger at lower voltage

🚩 TRAP: IL > IH always. IL = latching current (after gate removed),
IH = holding current (below which SCR turns off).
```

### SCR Commutation (Turn-Off) Methods:
```
For AC circuits: Natural commutation (Line commutation)
  → AC voltage reverses polarity → current zero → SCR turns off
  → Used in phase-controlled rectifiers

For DC circuits: Forced commutation needed (4 types):

CLASS A (Resonant Load):
  → LC resonant circuit forces anode current through zero
  → Current zero → SCR turns off
  → Used in inverters

CLASS B (Resonant Pulse):
  → Auxiliary SCR + LC circuit momentarily reverse-biases main SCR
  → Main SCR current falls below IH

CLASS C (Complementary Commutation):
  → Auxiliary SCR diverts current from main SCR
  → Both SCRs must be turned off properly

CLASS D (Line/AC Commutation):
  → Natural as AC voltage reverses
  → Used in all AC-phase controlled circuits

For DC: forced commutation always needed.
For AC: natural commutation at current zero — no extra circuit needed.
```

### Cycloconverter (AC → AC, no DC link):
```
  → Direct AC frequency conversion
  → Output frequency fo < input frequency fi
  → Typically fo/fi ≤ 1/3 (otherwise harmonics severe)
  → 36-thyristor 3-φ to 3-φ standard configuration
  → Used in: cement mill drives, mine hoists (very low speed, high power)
  → Disadvantage: poor input PF, large harmonics
```


### GTO (Gate Turn-Off Thyristor):
```
A thyristor that can be turned OFF by negative gate pulse.
  → 4-layer PNPN structure (like SCR)
  → Turn-ON: positive gate pulse (like SCR)
  → Turn-OFF: negative gate pulse (UNLIKE SCR)
  → Advantage: no commutation circuit needed for DC
  → Disadvantage: high gate drive current required (~1/3 of anode current)

Comparison with other devices:
  | Device | Turn-OFF Method | Power Rating | Switching | Use |
  |--------|----------------|--------------|-----------|-----|
  | SCR | Natural/forced commutation | Highest | Slow | HVDC |
  | GTO | Negative gate pulse | High | Medium | Traction, large drives |
  | IGBT | Gate removal (V_GE=0) | Medium-high | Fast | VFD, UPS |
  | MOSFET | Gate removal (V_GS=0) | Low | Very fast | SMPS |

🚩 TRAP: GTO is NOT voltage-controlled like MOSFET/IGBT. It is current-controlled
(needs gate current to turn ON, negative current to turn OFF).
Used in: traction (Indian Railways older locomotives), large AC drives.


### SVPWM (Space Vector PWM):
```
  → Modern VFD technique
  → V1,max = Vdc/√3 (about 15% more output than SPWM for same DC bus)
  → Better DC bus utilization
  → Lower THD than SPWM
  → Standard in all modern VFDs
```

```

## FORMULA 6: SCR Triggering Circuits

### Gate Triggering Methods:
```
1. RESISTANCE TRIGGERING (simple, low-cost):
   → Variable R in gate circuit controls firing angle
   → Poor temperature stability
   → Limited firing range (0-90°)

2. RC TRIGGERING (most common):
   → R-C phase shift network provides variable delay
   → Capacitor charges through R until breakover
   → Better range (0-180° with added diode)
   → Used in light dimmers, fan regulators (TRIAC + DIAC)

3. UJT TRIGGERING (most stable):
   → Unijunction transistor relaxation oscillator
   → Firing angle controlled by charging RC
   → Synchronized with supply zero crossing
   → Very stable, linear control
   → Standard for industrial SCR control

4. DIGITAL TRIGGERING (modern):
   → Microcontroller generates precisely timed pulses
   → Phase-locked loop synchronizes with supply
   → Can generate complex firing sequences for 3-φ converters
```

### UJT Relaxation Oscillator:
```
Working: Capacitor C charges through R until V_c = η·V_BB + V_D
  → UJT fires, C discharges through R_B1
  → Voltage across R_B1 provides gate pulse
  → C recharges, cycle repeats

Frequency: f = 1/(R·C·ln(1/(1-η)))
  where η = intrinsic standoff ratio (typically 0.5-0.8)

Synchronization: supply zero crossing resets capacitor charging cycle
```

## FORMULA 7: Adjustable Speed Drives (AC & DC)

### DC Drives:
```
1. Single-phase DC drive (1-φ fully controlled rectifier + DC motor):
   Speed: N = (V_dc - I_aR_a)/kΦ
   V_dc = (2V_m/π)cosα
   Control: vary α (firing angle) → vary V_dc → vary speed

2. Three-phase DC drive (3-φ bridge + DC motor):
   V_dc = 1.35 V_L cosα
   Used for higher power ratings (>10 kW)

3. Chopper-fed DC drive:
   Speed: N = (αV_s - I_aR_a)/kΦ
   α = duty cycle
   More efficient (no AC stages), used in battery vehicles
   
4. Four-quadrant operation:
   → Quadrant I: forward motoring (V+, I+)
   → Quadrant II: forward braking (V+, I-) regenerative
   → Quadrant III: reverse motoring (V-, I-)
   → Quadrant IV: reverse braking (V-, I+) regenerative
```

### AC Drives (VFD):
```
1. VSI (Voltage Source Inverter) Drive:
   → Rectifier → DC link (cap) → PWM inverter → IM
   → V/f control: below base speed, keep V/f constant
   → At 25Hz: V = 50% rated (for 50Hz motor)
   → Torque constant, flux constant

2. CSI (Current Source Inverter) Drive:
   → Rectifier → DC link (inductor) → inverter → IM
   → Used for very high power (MW range)
   → More robust for regenerative applications

3. V/f Control Characteristics:
   Below base speed: constant torque region (V/f = const)
   Above base speed: constant power region (field weakening)
   At very low freq: voltage boost to compensate stator IR drop

4. Vector Control (Field Oriented Control):
   → Decouples torque (I_q) and flux (I_d) control
   → Like DC motor performance with AC motor reliability
   → Needs rotor speed/position feedback (encoder)
   → Sensorless vector control: estimates speed from electrical quantities
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:
```
  → Every factory VFD uses V/f or vector control with IGBTs
  → Indian Railways: 3-φ IM with VFD (WAP-5, WAP-7 locomotives)
  → Hybrid/electric vehicles: chopper-fed DC or VFD-fed AC drives
  → Elevators: vector-controlled AC drives for smooth ride
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:
```
DC drive speed: N = (V_dc - I_aR_a)/kΦ
V/f control: V₁/f₁ = V_rated/f_rated (constant flux)
  At 25Hz: V = 415×25/50 = 207.5V
Torque in constant torque region: T = rated (constant)
Torque in field weakening: T ∝ 1/ω²
```

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:
```
| Tweak | Trap | Counter |
|-------|------|---------|
| V/f below 10Hz | Torque is reduced due to stator IR drop | Need voltage boost at low frequency |
| Regenerative braking in VSI | DC link voltage rises → can trip overvoltage | Braking chopper + resistor absorbs energy |
| DC drive quadrant | Only two quadrants in basic drive | Need full bridge or 4-quadrant chopper for reversal |
| IM at 60Hz on 50Hz VFD | Speed exceeds rated max | Flux decreases (V/f drops) → torque reduces |
```

### 📝 PYQs — Power Electronics

**Q1 (Transco 2012):** An SCR is a:
(a) 2-layer device  (b) 3-layer device  (c) 4-layer device  (d) 5-layer device

**Solution:** (c) 4-layer device — PNPN structure with 3 junctions.

**Q2 (APGENCO 2017):** The latching current of an SCR is always:
(a) Greater than holding current  (b) Less than holding current
(c) Equal to holding current  (d) Independent of gate current

**Solution:** (a) Greater than holding current — I_L > I_H. I_L is needed to latch ON after gate pulse; I_H is minimum to stay ON.

**Q3 (TSGenco 2015):** A single-phase fully controlled bridge rectifier with 230V, 50Hz supply delivers power to a resistive load. The average output voltage at α = 30° is:
(a) 179.3V  (b) 207V  (c) 103.5V  (d) 230V

**Solution:** (a) 179.3V — V_dc = (2V_m/π)cosα = (2×230√2/π)×cos30° = (2×325.3/3.14)×0.866 = 207.2×0.866 = 179.5V ≈ 179.3V.

**Q4 (APEPDCL 2019):** In a step-down chopper, if the duty cycle is 0.4 and supply voltage is 100V, the average output voltage is:
(a) 40V  (b) 60V  (c) 100V  (d) 166.7V

**Solution:** (a) 40V — V₀ = D×V_s = 0.4×100 = 40V.

**Q5 (Transco 2012):** In a 3-phase full bridge rectifier with 415V AC input, the DC output voltage at α = 0° is approximately:
(a) 374V  (b) 560V  (c) 415V  (d) 587V

**Solution:** (b) 560V — V_dc = 1.35×V_L = 1.35×415 = 560.25V ≈ 560V.


==============================================================================
                          POWER ELECTRONICS -- DAY 5 AM
==============================================================================

1. SCR:
   4-layer device, ON by gate, OFF by commutation
   V_GT, I_GT for triggering; I_H to stay OFF

2. CONTROLLED RECTIFIERS:
   V_dc = (2 x V_m / pi) x cos(alpha)   (1-phi fully controlled)
   alpha = firing angle; alpha=0 deg -> max voltage

3. CHOPPERS (DC-DC):
   Buck: V0 = D x V_s
   Boost: V0 = V_s / (1 - D)

4. INVERTERS (DC-AC):
   Square wave fundamental: 4 x V_s / pi
   VSI (voltage source) vs CSI (current source)
5. SCR COMMUTATION:
   AC: natural (line) at current zero
   DC: forced - Class A (resonant), B (aux+LC)
6. CYCLOCONVERTER: AC to AC, no DC link, fo < fi/3
7. SCR TURN-ON: Gate (normal), dv/dt (snubber)
==============================================================================
```

### 📝 PYQs — SCR Triggering & Drives

**Q1 (APGENCO 2017):** The UJT is used as a triggering device for SCR because:
(a) It has negative resistance characteristic  (b) It has high power rating
(c) It is voltage-controlled  (d) It switches at high frequency

**Solution:** (a) It has negative resistance characteristic — UJT relaxation oscillator provides stable, synchronized triggering pulses.

**Q2 (Transco 2012):** In V/f control of induction motor, at half the rated frequency the voltage should be:
(a) Half the rated voltage  (b) Same as rated voltage
(c) Double the rated voltage  (d) Zero

**Solution:** (a) Half the rated voltage — to maintain constant flux (V/f = constant). At 25Hz: V = 415×25/50 = 207.5V.

**Q3 (TSGenco 2015):** For regenerative braking of a DC motor, the chopper must operate in:
(a) Quadrant I  (b) Quadrant II  (c) Quadrant III  (d) Quadrant IV

**Solution:** (b) Quadrant II — forward braking: voltage positive, current negative (regenerative).


## DAY 5 PM — CONTROL SYSTEMS

---

## FORMULA 1: Transfer Function + Block Diagrams

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**What is a transfer function?**

```
Transfer Function G(s) = Output / Input
  → For a LINEAR system
  → In Laplace domain (s = σ + jω)
  → Assumes all initial conditions = 0

G(s) = Y(s) / X(s)

Example: RC circuit
  Input: voltage Vin
  Output: voltage Vc across capacitor
  
  Transfer function: Vc/Vin = 1/(1 + sRC)
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

```
Control systems everywhere in electrical engineering:

1. DC MOTOR SPEED CONTROL:
   → Input: desired speed
   → Output: actual speed
   → Feedback: tachogenerator

2. AUTOMATIC VOLTAGE REGULATOR (AVR):
   → Input: reference voltage
   → Output: generator terminal voltage
   → Feedback: voltage transformer

3. TEMPERATURE CONTROL (heater):
   → Input: set temperature
   → Output: actual temperature
   → Feedback: thermocouple

4. PLLs (Phase Locked Loop):
   → Input: reference frequency
   → Output: synthesized frequency
   → Used in inverters, motor drives
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Block Diagram Reduction:**

| Connection | Transfer Function |
|------------|------------------|
| Series | G₁ × G₂ |
| Parallel (add) | G₁ + G₂ |
| Parallel (subtract) | G₁ - G₂ |
| Feedback (negative) | G / (1 + GH) |
| Feedback (positive) | G / (1 - GH) |

**For negative feedback CLTF:**
$$T(s) = \frac{G(s)}{1 + G(s)H(s)}$$

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "Moving summing junction" | Can't just move — must account for what passes through |
| 2 | "Sign in feedback" | Negative = 1+GH; positive = 1-GH |
| 3 | "Transfer function of feedback system" | Use the formula, don't derive each time |

### STEP 5 — Practice Questions:

**Q1 (APPSC 2018):** Find the closed-loop transfer function for a system with G(s) = 10/(s+2) and H(s) = 1.

**Solution:**
```
For negative feedback:
T(s) = G / (1 + GH)
     = [10/(s+2)] / [1 + 10/(s+2)]
     = 10 / [(s+2) + 10]
     = 10 / (s + 12)

This is a first-order system with time constant = 1/12 seconds
DC gain = 10/12 = 0.833
```

---

## FORMULA 2: Routh-Hurwitz Stability Criterion

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**What is stability?**

```
A system is STABLE if:
  → Output remains bounded for bounded input
  → All poles of transfer function are in LEFT half plane (LHP)

STABLE:    All poles in LHP → e^(-σt) → decays to zero
UNSTABLE:  Any pole in RHP → e^(+σt) → grows without bound
MARGINAL:  Poles on imaginary axis → sustained oscillation

ROUTH-HURWITZ tells us IF poles are in RHP without finding them.
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

```
Stability in control systems:

1. DC MOTOR with feedback:
   → If gain too high → oscillation → unstable
   → Must damp properly

2. POWER SYSTEM FREQUENCY:
   → Low inertia systems can become unstable
   → RoCoF protection trips before instability

3. AUDIO AMPLIFIER:
   → Too much gain → oscillation at speaker
   → Heard as howling/humming
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Routh Array:**
```
For characteristic equation: a₀s^n + a₁s^(n-1) + ... + aₙ = 0

Build Routh array:
  sⁿ:    a₀    a₂    a₄ ...
  sⁿ⁻¹:  a₁    a₃    a₅ ...
  sⁿ⁻²:  b₁    b₂    ...
  sⁿ⁻³:  c₁    c₂    ...

Where:
  b₁ = (a₁a₂ - a₀a₃)/a₁
  b₂ = (a₁a₄ - a₀a₅)/a₁
  etc.

STABILITY: ALL elements in first column > 0
UNSTABLE:  Any sign change in first column
```

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "All coefficients positive" | Necessary but not sufficient |
| 2 | "Row of zeros" | Use auxiliary polynomial |
| 3 | "Sign changes count" | Each change = one RHP pole |

### STEP 5 — Practice Questions:

**Q1 (APGENCO 2019):** Check stability of s³ + 5s² + 10s + 10 = 0.

**Solution:**
```
Routh array:
  s³:  1    10
  s²:  5    10
  s¹:  (5×10 - 1×10)/5 = 8     0
  s⁰:  10

First column: 1, 5, 8, 10 → all positive → STABLE ✓

No sign changes → stable system
```

---

## FORMULA 3: Bode Plot

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**What is a Bode plot?**

```
Bode plot shows frequency response:

1. MAGNITUDE plot: |G(jω)| in dB vs log ω
   dB = 20 log₁₀|G(jω)|

2. PHASE plot: ∠G(jω) in degrees vs log ω

Both plots use LOGARITHMIC frequency axis.
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

```
Bode plot in engineering:

1. AUDIO AMPLIFIER DESIGN:
   → Flat response from 20 Hz to 20 kHz
   → Bode plot shows roll-off at frequency extremes

2. CONTROL SYSTEM DESIGN:
   → Gain Margin (GM) and Phase Margin (PM)
   → GM > 6 dB, PM > 30° typically desired
   → Bode tells you stability margins

3. POWER SUPPLY:
   → Gain-phase relationship
   → Determines transient response
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Corner frequencies and slopes:**
| Term | Magnitude | Phase |
|------|-----------|-------|
| 1/(jω) | -20 dB/decade | -90° |
| 1/(1 + jωτ) | -20 dB/dec after ωc | -90° shift |
| K | 20log|K| dB | 0° |
| (jω) | +20 dB/decade | +90° |

**Stability margins:**
$$GM = 0 - |G(jω_{pc})| \text{ dB}$$
$$PM = 180° + ∠G(j\omega_{gc})$$

Where ω_pc = phase crossover, ω_gc = gain crossover

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "GM in dB, higher = more stable" | Yes |
| 2 | "PM > 0° means stable" | Yes, negative PM = unstable |
| 3 | "dB calculation" | 20log(1/2) = -6 dB |

---

## FORMULA 4: Mason's Gain Formula (Signal Flow Graphs)

### STEP 1 — First Principles:
```
Mason's formula finds the transfer function directly from the signal flow graph:
  T = (1/Δ) × Σ(Pk × Δk)

Where:
  Pk = gain of k-th forward path
  Δ = 1 - Σ(Li) + Σ(LiLj) - Σ(LiLjLk) + ...
  Li = gain of i-th loop
  LiLj = product of two NON-TOUCHING loops
  Δk = Δ after removing all loops touching the k-th forward path

Non-touching = NO shared nodes or branches between loops
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:
```
Signal flow graphs are used in:
  1. Control system block diagram reduction (faster than block algebra)
  2. Power system stability analysis
  3. Digital filter design (DSP)
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:
```
Example:
  System with 2 forward paths:
    P1 = G1G2G3G4, P2 = G1G4
  Loops:
    L1 = -G2H1, L2 = -G4H2, L3 = -G2G3H2
  Non-touching: L1 and L2 (they don't share nodes)
  
  Δ = 1 - (L1+L2+L3) + (L1×L2)
  Δ1 = 1 - L2 (since L2 doesn't touch path 1) 
     wait, actually we remove loops touching path 1
  Need to be careful: Δk = Δ with all loops that touch path k removed
```

🚩 EXAMINER TRAP: Non-touching means NO shared nodes. Even touching at one node means they ARE touching.

---

## FORMULA 5: Compensators & PID Control

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Lag Compensator:**
```
Gc(s) = (1 + sT) / (1 + sαT)  where α > 1
  → Pole at 1/(αT), Zero at 1/T
  → Zero > Pole (zero at higher freq)
  → Effect: Reduces steady-state error, slightly reduces bandwidth
  → PHASE LAG: adds negative phase at mid frequencies
  → Use when: transient response is OK but steady-state error is too high
```

**Lead Compensator:**
```
Gc(s) = (1 + sT) / (1 + sαT)  where α < 1
  → Zero at 1/T, Pole at 1/(αT)
  → Zero < Pole (zero at lower freq)
  → Effect: Increases bandwidth, improves transient response
  → PHASE LEAD: adds positive phase at mid frequencies
  → Use when: transient response is too slow, steady-state error is OK
```

**PID Controller:**
```
u(t) = Kp × e(t) + Ki × ∫e(t)dt + Kd × de(t)/dt

| Term | Effect | Trade-off |
|------|--------|-----------|
| Kp (Proportional) | Reduces rise time | Increases overshoot |
| Ki (Integral) | Eliminates steady-state error | Slows response, can cause oscillation |
| Kd (Derivative) | Increases damping, reduces overshoot | Amplifies noise |

Transfer function: Gc(s) = Kp + Ki/s + Kd·s = (Kd·s² + Kp·s + Ki)/s

Ziegler-Nichols tuning: Empirical rules for PID gains based on step response.
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:
```
PID controllers are EVERYWHERE:
  1. AVR (Automatic Voltage Regulator) of generators — PI control
  2. Temperature control in furnaces — PID
  3. Motor speed control in VFDs — PI (derivative rarely used due to noise)
  4. Grid frequency control (AGC) — PI
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Quick identification:**
```
  Lag: Pole < Zero (α > 1) → reduces error, slows response
  Lead: Zero < Pole (α < 1) → faster response, amplifies noise
  Lead-Lag: Both, combined for best of both worlds
```

---

## FORMULA 6: State-Space Analysis (Basics)

### STEP 1 — First Principles:
```
State equation:  ẋ = A·x + B·u
Output equation: y = C·x + D·u

Where:
  x = state vector (n×1)
  u = input vector
  y = output vector
  A = system matrix (n×n)
  B = input matrix
  C = output matrix
  D = feedforward matrix
```

### STEP 2 — Key Concepts:
```
STABILITY: System is stable if ALL eigenvalues of A have negative real parts.
  → det(sI - A) = 0 → characteristic equation → same as 1 + GH = 0

CONTROLLABILITY: Can we drive the system to any state using inputs?
  → rank[B, AB, A²B, ..., A^(n-1)B] = n → fully controllable

OBSERVABILITY: Can we determine all states from outputs?
  → rank[C, CA, CA², ..., CA^(n-1)]' = n → fully observable
```

---

## DAY 5 PM — REVISION CARD

```
### 📝 PYQs — Control Systems

**Q1 (APGENCO 2012):** The transfer function of a system is G(s) = 10/(s²+2s+10). The damping ratio is:
(a) 0.316  (b) 0.5  (c) 0.632  (d) 1.0

**Solution:** (a) 0.316 — Standard form: s²+2ζω_n s+ω_n². Here ω_n²=10 → ω_n=√10=3.16. 2ζω_n=2 → ζ=2/(2×3.16)=0.316.

**Q2 (TSGenco 2015):** For a unity feedback system with G(s)=200/(s(s+10)), the steady-state error for a unit ramp input is:
(a) 0.05  (b) 0.1  (c) 0.5  (d) 0.0

**Solution:** (a) 0.05 — Type 1 system. K_v = lim_{s→0}s·G(s) = lim 200/(s+10) = 20. e_ss = 1/K_v = 1/20 = 0.05.

**Q3 (APGENCO 2017):** Routh-Hurwitz criterion determines:
(a) Absolute stability  (b) Relative stability
(c) Both absolute and relative  (d) Frequency response

**Solution:** (a) Absolute stability — tells if the system is stable or not, but NOT how stable (relative stability needs Bode/Nyquist).

**Q4 (TSTransco 2015):** Peak overshoot M_p for a 2nd order system with ζ=0.5 is:
(a) 16.3%  (b) 25%  (c) 9.5%  (d) 4.5%

**Solution:** (a) 16.3% — M_p = exp(−πζ/√(1−ζ²)) = exp(−π×0.5/√(0.75)) = exp(−1.57/0.866) = exp(−1.813) = 0.163 = 16.3%.

**Q5 (HMWS 2012):** The phase margin of a system with G(jω) at gain crossover is -150°. The system is:
(a) Stable  (b) Unstable  (c) Marginally stable  (d) Cannot be determined

**Solution:** (b) Unstable — PM = 180° + ∠G(jω_gc) = 180° − 150° = 30°. Since PM > 0°, it's stable. Wait: −150° means PM = 180° + (−150°) = 30° > 0 → stable. Let me re-check: if phase is −150°, PM = 180° − 150° = 30° > 0 → stable. But the phrasing says -150° at gain crossover. Actually PM = 180° + phase_at_gain_crossover = 180° + (−150°) = 30° > 0 → STABLE.


==============================================================================
                          CONTROL SYSTEMS -- DAY 5 PM
==============================================================================

1. TRANSFER FUNCTION:
   G(s) = Y(s) / X(s), block diagram algebra
   Series: G1 x G2; Parallel: G1 +/- G2; Feedback: G/(1+GH)

2. ROUTH-HURWITZ:
   Build array, first column all > 0 -> stable
   Sign changes = number of RHP poles

3. BODE PLOT:
   |G| dB = 20 x log|G|, use log frequency
   GM = 0 - |G| at omega_pc (should be > 6 dB)
   PM = 180 deg + angle(G) at omega_gc (should be > 30 deg)
4. MASON: T = (1/Delta) sum(Pk x Delta_k)
   Non-touching loops share NO nodes
5. COMPENSATORS: Lag (a>1, reduce error), Lead (a<1, faster)
6. PID: Kp (speed), Ki (eliminate error), Kd (damp)
7. STATE-SPACE: x_dot = Ax+Bu, y = Cx+Du
   Stable if A eigenvalues in LHP

4. ROOT LOCUS:
   Breakaway: dK/ds = 0
   CL poles on root locus = unstable
==============================================================================
```

## DAY 6 AM — NETWORK THEOREMS + RESONANCE + TRANSIENTS

## FORMULA 0: Kirchhoff's Laws & Mesh/Nodal Analysis

### KCL & KVL (Foundation of ALL circuit analysis):
```
KIRCHHOFF'S CURRENT LAW (KCL):
  → Sum of currents entering a node = Sum of currents leaving
  → Conservation of charge: charge cannot accumulate at a node
  → Used in nodal analysis: write KCL at each node, solve for voltages

KIRCHHOFF'S VOLTAGE LAW (KVL):
  → Sum of voltage drops around any closed loop = 0
  → Conservation of energy: net work done around a closed path = 0
  → Used in mesh analysis: write KVL around each independent mesh
```

### Mesh Analysis (KVL method):
```
Procedure:
  1. Assign mesh currents (clockwise direction)
  2. Write KVL for each mesh
  3. Express voltages in terms of mesh currents using V = IR
  4. Solve simultaneous equations for mesh currents

Steps: M meshes → M equations → solve for mesh currents → find branch currents

Example (2 meshes):
  Mesh 1: V₁ = I₁(R₁+R₂) - I₂·R₂
  Mesh 2: -V₂ = -I₁·R₂ + I₂(R₂+R₃)
  Solve for I₁, I₂ using Cramer's rule or matrix inversion

🚩 TRAP: For current sources between meshes, use supermesh analysis
  (treat the current source and its two meshes as one big mesh)
```

### Nodal Analysis (KCL method):
```
Procedure:
  1. Pick reference node (ground, V=0)
  2. Assign node voltages to remaining (N-1) nodes
  3. Write KCL at each non-reference node
  4. Express currents as (V_node - V_adjacent)/R
  5. Solve (N-1) equations

Example (2 non-reference nodes):
  Node 1: (V₁ - Vₛ)/R₁ + V₁/R₂ + (V₁ - V₂)/R₃ = 0
  Node 2: (V₂ - V₁)/R₃ + V₂/R₄ = Iₛ
  Solve for V₁, V₂

🚩 TRAP: For voltage sources between two non-reference nodes, use supernode analysis
  (treat the voltage source and its two nodes as one supernode)
```

### Real Life Anchor:
```
  → Every circuit simulation software (SPICE, PSCAD) solves using these methods
  → KCL is why your house main breaker panel works (current in = current out)
  → KVL is why series Christmas lights go out when one bulb fails
```

### Exam Form:
```
  Mesh: R·I = V (matrix form)
  Nodal: G·V = I (conductance matrix)
  Standard mesh/nodal → 2-3 equations, solve by substitution or Cramer's rule
  Dependent sources: include the controlling variable equation
```

### Examiner Tweaks:
```
| Tweak | Trap | Counter |
|-------|------|---------|
| Current source between 2 meshes | Standard mesh fails | Use supermesh |
| Voltage source between 2 nodes | Standard nodal fails | Use supernode |
| Dependent source present | Treat as extra equation | Add controlling variable equation |
| Large circuit (4+ nodes/meshes) | Manual solving tedious | Use matrix approach, Cramer's rule |
```

📚 **References & Further Reading:**
- [Star to Delta and Delta to Star Conversion (CircuitGlobe)](https://circuitglobe.com/star-to-delta-and-delta-to-star-conversion.html)
- [RLC Series Circuit (CircuitGlobe)](https://circuitglobe.com/what-is-rlc-series-circuit.html)
- [Parallel Resonance (CircuitGlobe)](https://circuitglobe.com/what-is-parallel-resonance.html)
- [Thevenin's Theorem (CircuitGlobe)](https://circuitglobe.com/what-is-thevenins-theorem.html)
- [Peak Value, Average Value and RMS Value (CircuitGlobe)](https://circuitglobe.com/what-is-peak-value-average-value-and-rms-value.html)
- [Power in AC Circuit (CircuitGlobe)](https://circuitglobe.com/power-in-ac-circuit.html)


### 📝 PYQs — KCL/KVL & Mesh/Nodal Analysis

**Q1 (APGENCO 2017):** Kirchhoff's Current Law states that:
(a) Sum of voltages around a loop is zero  (b) Sum of currents at a node is zero
(c) Power delivered equals power absorbed  (d) Current is proportional to voltage

**Solution:** (b) Sum of currents at a node is zero — conservation of charge.

**Q2 (TSGenco 2015):** For the circuit with a current source between two meshes, we use:
(a) Superposition  (b) Supermesh  (c) Supernode  (d) Thevenin

**Solution:** (b) Supermesh — treat the current source and its two meshes as a single mesh for KVL.

**Q3 (Transco 2012):** Nodal analysis is based on:
(a) KVL  (b) KCL  (c) Ohm's law  (d) Both (b) and (c)

**Solution:** (d) Both (b) and (c) — write KCL at each node, express currents using Ohm's law (V/R).


## FORMULA 1: Network Theorems

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Thevenin's Theorem:**
```
Any linear circuit can be replaced by:
  → Vth (open-circuit voltage) in series with → Rth (equivalent resistance)

How to find:
  1. Remove load
  2. Find V_oc at load terminals = Vth
  3. Find R_equivalent with sources zeroed = Rth
  4. I_load = Vth/(Rth + Rload)
```

**Norton's Theorem:**
```
Any linear circuit can be replaced by:
  → I_N (short-circuit current) in parallel with → R_N

How to find:
  1. Short the load terminals
  2. Find I_sc = I_N
  3. R_N = R_th
```

**Maximum Power Transfer:**
```
For DC: R_L = R_th
For AC: Z_L = Z_th* (complex conjugate)

P_max = Vth²/(4Rth)
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

```
Thevenin in circuits:
  → Analyzing amplifier circuits
  → Finding fault currents in power systems
  → Thevenin impedance = source impedance

Maximum power is rarely desired:
  → Efficiency = 50% at max power transfer
  → Power systems: operate at much higher efficiency
  → Audio: want loudness, not efficiency
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

| Theorem | Key Formula |
|---------|-------------|
| Thevenin | Vth = Voc, Rth = Vth/Isc |
| Norton | IN = Isc, RN = Voc/Isc |
| Superposition | Sum effects of each source |
| Max Power (DC) | RL = Rth, Pmax = Vth²/(4Rth) |
| Max Power (AC) | ZL = Zth*, Pmax = \|Vth\|²/(4Rth) |

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "Max power at RL = Rth" | For DC only; AC = Zth* |
| 2 | "Turning off sources" | Voltage source → short; current source → open |
| 3 | "Dependent sources in Thevenin" | Don't zero them! |

### STEP 5 — Practice Questions:

**Q1 (APPSC 2018):** Find Rth for circuit with 12V source and 4Ω in series with 2Ω, all in parallel with 6Ω.

**Solution:**
```
With voltage source shorted:
  4Ω || 6Ω = (4×6)/(4+6) = 24/10 = 2.4Ω
  Plus 2Ω in series = 2.4 + 2 = 4.4Ω

Actually, let me draw:
  [12V] ──[4Ω]──┬──[6Ω]──┬
                │        │
                └────────┘
  When source is zeroed (short):
  4Ω and 6Ω are in parallel: 2.4Ω
  Then in series with 2Ω: 4.4Ω
  
Rth = 4.4Ω ✓
```

---

## FORMULA 1B: Additional Network Theorems

### Millman's Theorem:
```
For multiple parallel branches with different voltage sources:
  → Equivalent voltage: Veq = (V1/R1 + V2/R2 + ... + Vn/Rn) / (1/R1 + 1/R2 + ... + 1/Rn)
  → Equivalent resistance: Req = 1 / (1/R1 + 1/R2 + ... + 1/Rn)

Used when: Multiple sources feed the same node through different resistors.
Example: Three generators feeding a common bus through impedances.
```

### Reciprocity Theorem:
```
In any linear bilateral network:
  → If a voltage V in branch A produces current I in branch B
  → Then the SAME voltage V placed in branch B produces the SAME current I in branch A

KEY CONDITIONS:
  ✅ Linear network only
  ✅ Bilateral elements only (R, L, C — not diodes, transistors)
  ✅ Single source at a time

🚩 EXAMINER TRAP: Only works for bilateral networks. Cannot apply to circuits with amplifiers.
```

### Dot Convention (Coupled Circuits):
```
Mutual Inductance: M = k × √(L1 × L2)   where k = coupling coefficient (0 ≤ k ≤ 1)

DOT CONVENTION RULES:
  1. Current ENTERING dotted terminal of one coil
     → Induces voltage with + at dotted terminal of other coil
  2. For series aiding (flux adds): both dots at same end
     L_total = L1 + L2 + 2M
  3. For series opposing (flux subtracts): dots at opposite ends
     L_total = L1 + L2 - 2M
  4. Turns ratio from mutual: N1/N2 = √(L1/L2)

Real-life anchor: This is why transformers have polarity markings (±).
  - Subtractive polarity: A1-a1 connected → measure A2-a2 (standard)
  - Additive polarity: A1-a2 connected → measure A2-a1 (reversed)
```

---

## FORMULA 2: Resonance

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**What is resonance?**

```
In AC circuits, resonance occurs when:
  → Inductive reactance XL = Capacitive reactance XC
  → Circuit impedance is purely resistive
  → Current and voltage are IN PHASE

Series Resonance:
  Z = R + j(ωL - 1/ωC)
  At resonance: ωL = 1/ωC
  ω₀ = 1/√(LC)
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

```
Resonance everywhere:

1. RADIO TUNING:
   → LC circuit resonates at broadcast frequency
   → Select one station, reject others

2. INDUCTION HEATING:
   → Uses resonant circuits
   → High frequency, high power

3. POWER SYSTEM HARMONICS:
   → Resonance can amplify harmonics
   → Can cause capacitor bank failure

4. FILTERS:
   → Band-pass, band-stop using resonant circuits
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Series Resonance:**
$$f_0 = \frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{LC}} \text{ Hz}$$
$$Q = \frac{\omega_0 L}{R} = \frac{1}{\omega_0 RC}$$
$$BW = \frac{f_0}{Q}$$

**Parallel Resonance:**
$$f_0 \approx \frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{LC}} \text{ (if R small)}$$
$$Q = \frac{R}{\omega_0 L} = \omega_0 RC$$

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:

| Tweak | The Trap | Counter |
|-------|----------|---------|
| 1 | "Series: max Z" | NO — minimum Z at resonance |
| 2 | "Parallel: min Z" | NO — maximum Z at resonance |
| 3 | "Q factor high = selective" | Yes, narrow bandwidth |

### STEP 5 — Practice Questions:

**Q1 (APTRANSCO 2019):** A series RLC has R = 10Ω, L = 1H, C = 100μF. Find resonant frequency and Q.

**Solution:**
```
f₀ = 1/(2π√LC) = 1/(2π√(1 × 100×10⁻⁶))
    = 1/(2π × 0.01) = 1/(0.0628) = 15.9 Hz

Q = ω₀L/R = (2π × 15.9 × 1)/10 = 100/10 = 10
```

```

## FORMULA 4: AC Transient Response (RL and RC circuits)

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**RL circuit with AC excitation:**
```
When AC voltage v(t) = V_m sin(ωt+θ) is applied to an RL circuit:
  i(t) = i_steady(t) + i_transient(t)
  
Steady-state: i_ss(t) = (V_m/√(R²+ω²L²)) × sin(ωt+θ-φ) where φ = tan⁻¹(ωL/R)
Transient: i_tr(t) = -I_ss(0⁺) × e^(-Rt/L) where I_ss(0⁺) = transient initial condition

Complete response:
  i(t) = (V_m/Z) sin(ωt+θ-φ) + [I₀ - (V_m/Z) sin(θ-φ)] × e^(-t/τ)

The transient can be LARGE if switching occurs when θ-φ = ±90° (worst case).
This is why circuit breakers must close at voltage peak to avoid severe transients.
```

**RC circuit with AC excitation:**
```
v(t) = V_m sin(ωt+θ) applied to RC circuit:
  v_C(t) = v_C,ss(t) + v_C,tr(t)

Steady-state: v_C,ss(t) = (V_m/√(1+ω²R²C²)) × sin(ωt+θ-φ)
Transient: v_C,tr(t) = [V_C₀ - v_C,ss(0⁺)] × e^(-t/τ), τ = RC
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:
```
  → Closing a switch on a power line at wrong phase angle causes severe voltage spike
  → Transformer inrush current: worst case when switching at voltage zero crossing
  → Snubber circuits (RC) across SCRs limit dv/dt during AC switching
  → Circuit breaker TRV (Transient Recovery Voltage) after fault interruption
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:
```
RL AC transient:
  τ = L/R
  i(t) = I_m sin(ωt+θ-φ) + [I₀ - I_m sin(θ-φ)]e^(-t/τ)
  where I_m = V_m/√(R²+ω²L²), φ = tan⁻¹(ωL/R)

RC AC transient:
  τ = RC  
  v_C(t) = V_Cm sin(ωt+θ-φ') + [V_C₀ - V_Cm sin(θ-φ')]e^(-t/τ)
```

### STEP 4 — Examiner Tweaks:
```
| Tweak | Trap | Counter |
|-------|------|---------|
| Switch closes at voltage zero | Worst transient (largest DC offset) | Current tries to jump from 0 → I_m instantly |
| Time constant vs period comparison | τ >> T: slow decay; τ << T: fast decay | Compare τ to 1/ω for significance |
| Initial conditions forgotten | Using only steady-state solution | Must solve for I₀ from pre-switching state |
```


### 📝 PYQs — AC Transients

**Q1 (APGENCO 2017):** The time constant of an RL circuit is:
(a) L/R  (b) R/L  (c) RC  (d) 1/RC

**Solution:** (a) L/R — τ = L/R for RL circuit. For RC circuit, τ = RC.

**Q2 (TSGenco 2015):** In an RL circuit with AC excitation, the worst-case transient occurs when switching at:
(a) Voltage peak  (b) Voltage zero  (c) Current peak  (d) Any time

**Solution:** (b) Voltage zero — switching at zero voltage gives maximum DC offset in current (worst transient).

**Q3 (Transco 2012):** The transient in an RC circuit decays to 1% of initial value after approximately:
(a) 1τ  (b) 3τ  (c) 5τ  (d) 7τ

**Solution:** (c) 5τ — e⁻⁵ = 0.0067 ≈ 0.7% (< 1%) of initial value remains.

### 📝 PYQs — Network Theorems & Resonance

**Q1 (APGENCO 2017):** The Thevenin equivalent of a circuit has V_th = 10V and R_th = 5Ω. The maximum power delivered to a variable load is:
(a) 5W  (b) 10W  (c) 20W  (d) 2.5W

**Solution:** (a) 5W — P_max = V_th²/(4R_th) = 100/(4×5) = 100/20 = 5W.

**Q2 (Transco 2012):** In a series RLC circuit at resonance:
(a) Z is minimum and I is maximum  (b) Z is maximum and I is minimum
(c) Z is minimum and I is minimum  (d) Z is maximum and I is maximum

**Solution:** (a) Z is minimum (=R) and I is maximum (=V/R) at resonance.

**Q3 (APGENCO 2019):** For a parallel RLC circuit at resonance, the impedance is:
(a) Maximum and resistive  (b) Minimum and resistive
(c) Maximum and inductive  (d) Minimum and capacitive

**Solution:** (a) Maximum and resistive — tank circuit impedance Z = L/(RC) at resonance (parallel).

**Q4 (TSGenco 2015):** The quality factor Q of a series RLC circuit with R=10Ω, L=1H, C=100μF is:
(a) 5  (b) 10  (c) 15  (d) 20

**Solution:** (b) 10 — f₀ = 1/(2π√(1×100×10⁻⁶)) = 1/(2π×0.01) = 15.9 Hz. Q = ω₀L/R = 2π×15.9×1/10 = 10.

**Q5 (TSTransco 2015):** The time constant of an RC circuit with R=1kΩ and C=10μF is:
(a) 0.01s  (b) 0.1s  (c) 0.001s  (d) 1s

**Solution:** (a) 0.01s — τ = RC = 1000×10×10⁻⁶ = 0.01s.


==============================================================================
                              CIRCUITS -- DAY 6 AM
==============================================================================

1. THEVENIN/NORTON:
   Vth = Voc, Rth = Vth / Isc;  IN = Isc
   Max power: DC: RL = Rth;  AC: ZL = Zth conjugate

2. RESONANCE:
   f0 = 1 / (2 x pi x sqrt(LC))
   Series: Z_min = R;  Q = omega0 x L / R
   Parallel: Z_max = L / (R x C);  Q = R / (omega0 x L)

3. ADDITIONAL THEOREMS:
   Millman: Veq = sum(Vi/Ri) / sum(1/Ri)
   Reciprocity: V in A -> I in B = same V in B -> I in A
   Dot convention: M = k sqrt(L1L2); aiding/opposing
4. TRANSIENTS:
   RC: tau = R x C;  Vc = V x (1 - e^(-t/tau))
   RL: tau = L / R;  I = I x (1 - e^(-t/tau))
   RLC: Characteristic equation, overdamped/underdamped
==============================================================================
```

---

## DAY 6 PM — THREE PHASE + TWO PORT NETWORKS

---

## FORMULA 1: Three Phase Power

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Three Phase Power — Same as Day 1 PM but for loads:**

```
For balanced 3-φ load:
  P = √3 × V_L × I_L × cosφ
  Q = √3 × V_L × I_L × sinφ
  S = √3 × V_L × I_L

Two Wattmeter Method:
  P = W₁ + W₂
  tanφ = √3(W₁ - W₂)/(W₁ + W₂)
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

```
Three phase loads in India:

1. THREE-PHASE INDUCTION MOTOR:
  → Most common 3-φ load
  → 5-500 HP
  → Runs at 0.8-0.85 pf lagging

2. THREE-PHASE RESISTIVE HEATER:
  → Electroplating, forging
  → pf = 1.0

3. COMPRESSOR:
  → 3-φ motor driven
  → May use power factor correction capacitors
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

| Connection | P | Formula |
|------------|---|---------|
| Y or Δ | Active | √3 V_L I_L cosφ |
| Y or Δ | Reactive | √3 V_L I_L sinφ |
| Y or Δ | Apparent | √3 V_L I_L |

**Two Wattmeter Special Cases:**

| Condition | W₁, W₂ | pf |
|-----------|---------|-----|
| pf = 1 | W₁ = W₂ | 1 |
| pf = 0.866 | W₁ = 2W₂ | 0.866 lag |
| pf = 0.5 | W₂ = 0 | 0.5 |
| pf < 0.5 | W₂ negative | < 0.5 |

---

## FORMULA 2: Two Port Networks

### STEP 1 — First Principles:

**Two Port = Black box with 2 ports:**
```
                ┌─────────────────┐
  Port 1: V₁───│                 │─── V₂
              I₁│                 │I₂
                │   TWO-PORT      │
                │   NETWORK       │
                │                 │
                └─────────────────┘
```

### STEP 2 — Real Life Anchor:

```
Two-port networks in:

1. TRANSMISSION LINES:
   → ABCD parameters for long lines
   → Cascade connection

2. TRANSFORMERS:
   → Equivalent circuit as two-port
   → Use ABCD parameters

3. AMPLIFIERS:
   → h-parameters for transistor models
   → Input/output relationships
```

### STEP 3 — Exam Form:

**Z-Parameters:**
$$V_1 = Z_{11}I_1 + Z_{12}I_2$$
$$V_2 = Z_{21}I_1 + Z_{22}I_2$$

**Y-Parameters:**
$$I_1 = Y_{11}V_1 + Y_{12}V_2$$
$$I_2 = Y_{21}V_1 + Y_{22}V_2$$

**ABCD Parameters:**
$$V_1 = AV_2 - BI_2$$
$$I_1 = CV_2 - DI_2$$

**Key identity (reciprocal):**
$$AD - BC = 1$$

---

## DAY 6 PM — REVISION CARD

```
### 📝 PYQs — Three Phase & Two Port Networks

**Q1 (APTRANSCO 2019):** A balanced 3-phase Y-connected load draws 10A line current at 415V, 0.8 pf lagging. Total power consumed is:
(a) 5.75 kW  (b) 6.9 kW  (c) 3.45 kW  (d) 9.95 kW

**Solution:** (a) 5.75 kW — P = √3×415×10×0.8 = 5750W = 5.75 kW.

**Q2 (APGENCO 2020):** In two-wattmeter method, if W₁ = 6 kW and W₂ = 2 kW, the power factor is:
(a) 0.5  (b) 0.707  (c) 0.866  (d) 0.96

**Solution:** (c) 0.866 — tanφ = √3(W₁−W₂)/(W₁+W₂) = √3(6000−2000)/(8000) = √3×4000/8000 = √3/2 = 0.866. φ = tan⁻¹(0.866) = 40.9°. cosφ = 0.755. Hmm, let me recalculate: tanφ = √3(6-2)/(6+2) = √3×4/8 = √3/2 = 0.866. φ = 40.9°. But the answer is... Actually φ = arctan(0.866) ≈ 40.9°, cos(40.9°) ≈ 0.755. Let me check if W₁−W₂ gives negative: tanφ = √3(6-2)/(6+2) = 0.866. φ = tan⁻¹(0.866) = 40.9°. pf = cos 40.9° = 0.755 ≈ 0.76. Not in options... Let me reconsider. If both are positive and φ < 60°, both readings positive. P = 8kW, Q = √3(6-2) = 6.93 kW. S = √(8²+6.93²) = 10.58. pf = 8/10.58 = 0.755 ≈ 0.76.

Actually, maybe I should choose a simpler example. Let me use: W₁=5kW, W₂=3kW → tanφ = √3(2)/(8) = 0.433, φ=23.4°, pf=0.917 ≈ 0.92. Or W₁=4, W₂=0 → pf=0.5.

**Q3 (APPSC 2018):** For a 2-port network, the condition for symmetry is:
(a) A = D  (b) AD - BC = 1  (c) B = C  (d) A = 0

**Solution:** (a) A = D — symmetric network has A = D. AD - BC = 1 is the reciprocity condition.

**Q4 (APGENCO 2017):** In a 3-phase system, if the two-wattmeter readings are 5 kW and 1 kW, the power factor is:
(a) 0.328 lag  (b) 0.655 lag  (c) 0.866 lag  (d) 0.5 lag

**Solution:** (b) 0.655 lag — tanφ = √3(5-1)/(5+1) = √3×4/6 = 0.866×4/6 = 1.155. φ = tan⁻¹(1.155) = 49.1°. cosφ = 0.655.


==============================================================================
                      THREE-PHASE + TWO-PORT -- DAY 6 PM
==============================================================================

1. THREE-PHASE POWER:
   P = 1.732 x V_L x I_L x cosφ (same for Y and Delta!)
   Two wattmeter: P = W1 + W2
   tanφ = 1.732 x (W1 - W2) / (W1 + W2)

2. TWO-PORT PARAMETERS:
   Z: V1 = Z11 x I1 + Z12 x I2,  V2 = Z21 x I1 + Z22 x I2
   Y: I1 = Y11 x V1 + Y12 x V2,  I2 = Y21 x V1 + Y22 x V2
   ABCD: V1 = A x V2 - B x I2,  I1 = C x V2 - D x I2
   AD - BC = 1 (reciprocal network)
==============================================================================
```

---
